Citizen Brief · Appendices · citizen-v2.0 · Water in Nevada · The Nevada Forum · July 2026

Growth, Water Scarcity, and Long-Term Supply in Nevada — Appendices

Long-form detail behind the two-page citizen brief. 165 bills found for 2019–2025; 100 policy bills carry the headline numbers.

Contents
  1. Appendix A — Every bill in the set — Every bill: plain topic, theme, result, where it ended, sponsors
  2. Appendix B — Theme scorecards — Theme scorecards with baskets and stop patterns
  3. Appendix C — Votes and support — Final Passage votes, party splits, high-support non-enactments
  4. Appendix D — Sponsors and people — Sponsors and people
  5. Appendix E — How far each bill got — Step-by-step progress for every policy bill
  6. Appendix F — What this data can and cannot say — What this data can and cannot say
  7. Appendix G — Did the text change on the way to the governor? — Introduced vs. final text for governor-bound bills
  8. Appendix H — Citizen statements, checked against the record — The ten most common citizen statements, each checked against the record
  9. Appendix I — Sources and review notes — Claim-to-source mapping, collection notes, review status

Committee Yea votes marked * are inferred (committee membership minus recorded Nay/Absent), because Nevada minutes usually list only No and Absent votes.

Appendix A — Every bill in the set

This record holds 772 bills found for 2019–2025: 409 policy bills (healthcare cost, coverage, access, or provider-supply policy is a real part of the bill) and 363 context bills (found by broad search terms or omnibus indexing; kept so the search is auditable). Headline numbers in the front brief use the policy set only. “Where it ended” tells you the last step the bill reached — the record never says why it stopped.

Appendix B — Theme scorecards

Each policy bill was assigned one theme. The basket says how this kind of idea usually fared in this record: Often moved before / Got support but didn’t finish / Rarely moved before / Mixed.

Licensing doctors, nurses, and other providers: compacts, endorsement, scope of practice

  • Bills: 90 · Became law: 49 (54.4%) · Basket: mixed · Certainty: high
  • Typical stop: first committee (22 of 41 non-enactments); the Nurse Licensure Compact specifically died in its first committee in 2021, 2023, and 2025
  • Inference: profession-by-profession compacts and endorsement bills pass with unanimous or near-unanimous floor votes; the nurse compact is the exception that never reaches a floor.

Prescription drug costs, pharmacies, and pharmacy benefit managers

  • Bills: 35 · Became law: 15 (42.9%) · Basket: Majority floor votes, both-chamber passage, or strong momentum without enactment · Certainty: high
  • Typical stop: first committee (10); the two price-cap bills that reached the desk were vetoed (82:AB250, 83:AB259); full PBM regulation died late twice (82:SB352, 83:SB316)
  • Inference: transparency and Medicaid-side PBM bills pass; bills that cap prices or regulate PBMs across the commercial market stop late - in the second house, the amendment shuttle, or at the governor’s desk.

  • Bills: 22 · Became law: 8 (36.4%) · Basket: Repeated early stops (especially the first committee) for this kind of idea · Certainty: high
  • Typical stop: first committee (7); vetoed at the desk when it got there (83:AB204 medical debt, 82:AB437 paperwork fees, 83:AB282 billing errors)
  • Inference: the 2019 out-of-network emergency billing law is the standout; since then, patient-cost bills have the lowest pass rate of any theme and drew three of the record’s fifteen vetoes.

Appendix F — What this data can and cannot say

Plain-language limits of the record behind this packet.

  1. The set was found by keyword search plus the official subject index; it is broad but not guaranteed complete.
  2. The record shows where each bill stopped — never why.
  3. Committee Yea votes are inferred (membership minus recorded Nay/Absent) because Nevada minutes usually list only No and Absent votes; these rows are marked in the source data.
  4. No OpenStates data in this refresh; party labels come from official NELIS legislator directories and legislator pages (97.7% of roll-call ballot rows matched - unmatched rows are minutes-parsing name fragments left unlabeled; sponsor party coverage is 100%).
  5. Context bills (found by broad search terms or omnibus indexing) are kept for audit but excluded from headline counts.

Known collection gaps recorded during the refresh: 773 items (see sources/nevada/cost-of-living/processed/data-gaps.json). OpenStates was unavailable for this refresh; all history, votes, and party labels come from the official NELIS system and legislator rosters, with committee votes read from official minutes PDFs.

Appendix H — Citizen statements, checked against the record

The ten most common citizen statements on the rising cost of living (healthcare focus) from the Phase 2 Community Conversations (RAG constituent-voice dataset), each checked against the 2019–2025 bill record. “Never filed” means no bill in this record proposed it — not that it is impossible.

1. Adopt interstate licensure compacts (e.g., the Nurse Licensure Compact) to expand provider supply.

  • Status: Tried; passed for some professions, stalled for nurses
  • The record: Compacts pass easily for some professions and keep dying for nurses. Enacted, all with unanimous or near-unanimous floor votes: the Physical Therapy Compact (2019 SB186; re-enacted 2025 AB248, 41-1 and 21-0), the EMS personnel compact (2023 AB158, 42-0 and 21-0), the Audiology and Speech-Language Pathology Compact (2025 AB230, 41-0 and 21-0), and licensure by endorsement for dental hygienists (2025 AB334, 42-0 and 21-0). The Nurse Licensure Compact - the compact citizens named - has been filed three times and never reached a floor vote: 2021 AB142 (a Republican sponsor team) and 2023 AB108 both died in Assembly Commerce and Labor, and in 2025 it was folded into SB34, a five-compact omnibus (PA, nurse, audiology/SLP, PT, OT) that died in Senate Commerce and Labor while the audiology/SLP and PT compacts passed as separate bills. For physicians, the Interstate Medical Licensure Compact already sits in Nevada statute from before this record’s window - SB34’s own digest places the PA compact ‘in the same chapter as the Interstate Medical Licensure Compact’ - and the record’s physician measures ease individual licensure further: 2025 SB124 (foreign medical graduates’ path to full licensure) passed 41-0 and 21-0, while the 2019 provisional-license design (SB259) died on the Senate floor.
  • Where the decision sits: Fully a state-legislature lever: compacts are enacted as state statutes. The 25-year multi-state findings participants cited are not in this dataset; what the Nevada record shows is the pattern of which compacts reached a floor.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 SB186 Joined an interstate compact letting physical therapists licensed elsewhere practice in Nevada. Enacted Became law
2025 AB248 Joined an interstate compact letting physical therapists licensed in member states practice in Nevada. Enacted Became law
2021 SB100 Joins an interstate compact letting physical therapists licensed elsewhere practice in Nevada. Failed Died in the second house
2023 SB97 Joins an interstate compact letting licensed physical therapists practice across member states. Failed Died in the second house
2023 AB158 Joined an interstate compact letting emergency medical personnel certified elsewhere work in Nevada. Enacted Became law
2021 AB386 Joins an interstate compact letting emergency medical personnel licensed elsewhere work in Nevada. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 AB230 Joined an interstate compact letting audiologists and speech-language pathologists licensed elsewhere practice in Nevada. Enacted Became law
2025 AB106 Joins an interstate compact letting licensed occupational therapists practice across member states. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 AB334 Eased licensure by endorsement for out-of-state dental hygienists and expanded function dental assistants. Enacted Became law
2021 AB142 Joined the Nurse Licensure Compact so nurses licensed elsewhere can practice in Nevada. Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB108 Joined the Nurse Licensure Compact so nurses licensed in other member states can practice in Nevada. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB34 Join the interstate PA Licensure Compact so physician assistants licensed elsewhere can practice in Nevada. Failed Died in its first committee
2019 SB259 Let out-of-state doctors get quick provisional Nevada licenses when sponsored by a licensed Nevada physician. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 SB124 Eased the path for foreign medical school graduates to move from restricted to full physician licenses. Enacted Became law
2019 SB482 Addressed interstate health care choice compacts letting insurers sell plans across state lines. Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - What objection stops the nurse compact in committee while the PT, EMS, and audiology compacts pass unanimously - and is it different in Assembly versus Senate Commerce and Labor? - Would a stand-alone NLC bill fare differently than the 2025 omnibus packaging? - The physician compact is already in Nevada statute - is the licensure barrier participants described a compact gap at all, or a board-processing one?

2. Increase the overall supply of physicians and providers.

  • Status: Tried; finished in the special session
  • The record: The largest supply bill in the record became law three months late. 2025 SB434 (Senate Majority Leader Cannizzaro) created the Statewide Health Care Access and Recruitment Grant Program - competitive grants against critical provider shortages - passed the Senate 18-2 and the Assembly 42-0 as amended, and died on the session’s last day when the Senate did not concur in the Assembly amendment. The 36th (2025) Special Session then enacted the same program as SB5 (15-6 and 37-0, Chapter 12), housing it under the new Nevada Health Authority. Around it sits a steady record: Health Service Corps funding enacted in 2019 (SB289) and 2021 (SB233), dental therapy licensed in 2019 (SB366), workforce-data laws enacted in 2021 (AB278, SB379). The failures cluster at the money stage: the 2023 Health Service Corps refill (AB248) died in committee, the workforce-needs assessment (2025 SB495) passed the Senate 13-8 and died in the Assembly, and a proposed health-workforce office (2025 SB425) died on the Senate floor.
  • Where the decision sits: A state-legislature lever, and now an existing program: the Statewide Health Care Access and Recruitment Grant Program is in statute with an account under the Nevada Health Authority. The ‘roughly 500 doctors’ gap participants cited is not measured in this dataset.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2025 SB434 Create a state grant program to fix critical shortages of health care providers and clinical services. Failed Passed both houses, still no law
2019 SB289 Gave money to the Nevada Health Service Corps to attract medical providers to underserved areas. Enacted Became law
2021 SB233 Funds the Nevada Health Service Corps to attract medical and dental providers to underserved areas. Enacted Became law
2019 SB366 Created the new licensed profession of dental therapy to expand the dental workforce. Enacted Became law
2021 AB278 Had the state collect workforce data from physicians when they register or renew licenses. Enacted Became law
2021 SB379 Creates a state database of health provider demographics and practice details for workforce planning. Enacted Became law
2023 AB248 Funded the Nevada Health Service Corps to attract health practitioners to underserved areas. Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB37 Created a university center to grow Nevada’s behavioral health workforce. Enacted Became law
2025 SB495 Require a regular statewide assessment of health care workforce needs and shortages. Failed Died in the second house
2025 SB425 Create a state office to oversee health care workforce needs and provider licensing. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 AB413 Created a Southern Nevada apprenticeship pilot to train medical assistants and pharmacy technicians amid shortages. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar

Open questions for a working group: - The grant program is now law - is the next question its funding level rather than a new bill? - What did the Assembly’s Amendment 972 change in SB434, and does SB5 as enacted reflect it? - Does the new Nevada Health Authority placement change who decides where grants go?

3. Fund more graduate medical education / residency programs.

  • Status: Precedent exists
  • The record: A state residency-grant program is already in statute: 2023 SB350 created grants for new and expanded physician residency programs (passed 21-0 and 40-0) and 2025 SB262 revised it (21-0 and 42-0) - both carried by Sen. Julie Pazina (D) with co-sponsors from both parties’ orbit. What keeps failing is the funding design that runs through tax credits: Sen. Robin Titus’s (R) bills giving businesses credits for donations to residency programs died on the Senate floor in 2023 (SB369) and 2025 (SB269), and the 2019 direct appropriation for postgraduate residencies (AB311, Krasner R) died in its first committee. The pathway bills pass: 2025 SB124 (Doñate D) eased foreign medical graduates’ path to full licensure, 41-0 and 21-0. The combined debt-plus-residency design (2023 AB393, Kasama R + Lange D) died in its first committee, and 2025 AB170’s associate-physician licenses died the same way.
  • Where the decision sits: A state-legislature lever with a program already on the books; residency slots also depend on federal Medicare GME funding, which no Nevada bill controls - the state bills fund state grants on top.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2023 SB350 Creates a state grant program funding new and expanded physician residency training programs. Enacted Became law
2025 SB262 Revise the state grant program that funds physician residency and fellowship training positions. Enacted Became law
2023 SB369 Gives businesses a tax credit for donations that fund new hospital residency programs in shortage specialties. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 SB269 Give businesses a tax credit for donating to graduate medical education residency programs. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2019 AB311 Appropriated state money for postgraduate medical education residency programs. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB124 Eased the path for foreign medical school graduates to move from restricted to full physician licenses. Enacted Became law
2023 AB393 Created a program repaying doctors’ student debt and funding residencies to attract physicians to Nevada. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 AB170 Created limited licenses letting medical school graduates practice under supervision as associate physicians. Failed Died in its first committee
2023 SB204 Lets certain medical school graduates practice medicine in limited settings before full licensure. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 SB40 Create a Medicaid account to fund residency training, nursing loans and other health workforce programs. Failed Died in its first committee

Open questions for a working group: - SB350/SB262 grants exist - what has the program funded so far, and is the constraint appropriations rather than statute? - Why does the tax-credit funding design die on the Senate floor when the same goal passes as a grant program?

4. Offer student-loan / medical-debt forgiveness for providers serving in-state or in rural areas.

  • Status: Already in statute and expanding
  • The record: Participants described this as not yet drafted; the record shows it is Nevada’s most consistently enacted recruitment tool. The Nevada Health Service Corps - loan repayment for providers who commit to underserved areas - was funded in 2019 (SB289, 41-0 and 21-0) and 2021 (SB233, 42-0 and 19-0), both carried by Sen. Joseph Hardy (R). 2023 AB45 created a broader program repaying student loans of health providers working in underserved communities (41-0 and 21-0). In 2025 the Legislature expanded eligibility twice: AB269 added speech-language pathologists, physical therapists and others (40-2 and 21-0), and SB266 widened qualifying provider types again (42-0 and 21-0). The failures are the bigger-money versions: the 2019 stand-alone loan-repayment program (AB358, Republican sponsors) died on the origin floor, the 2021 physician-stipend design (AB372, Kasama R) died in committee, the 2023 Health Service Corps refill (AB248) died in committee, and the behavioral-health expansion (AB69) died on the origin floor in 2023.
  • Where the decision sits: A state-legislature lever with the machinery already in statute; the rural-funding tie-in participants floated (federal rural money as the source) does not appear in any bill in this record.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 SB289 Gave money to the Nevada Health Service Corps to attract medical providers to underserved areas. Enacted Became law
2021 SB233 Funds the Nevada Health Service Corps to attract medical and dental providers to underserved areas. Enacted Became law
2023 AB45 Created a program repaying student loans of health providers working in underserved communities. Enacted Became law
2025 AB269 Added speech-language pathologists, physical therapists, and others to the health provider student loan repayment program. Enacted Became law
2025 SB266 Let more types of health providers qualify for student loan repayment for working in underserved areas. Enacted Became law
2019 AB358 Created a loan repayment program to attract doctors and health professionals to practice in Nevada. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2021 AB372 Creates a program repaying student debt and paying stipends to attract physicians to Nevada. Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB248 Funded the Nevada Health Service Corps to attract health practitioners to underserved areas. Failed Died in its first committee
2023 AB69 Expands loan repayment for health providers in shortage areas to include behavioral health providers. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar

Open questions for a working group: - The programs exist - what are their current appropriations and how many providers do they actually place? - Would a service-commitment design tied to medical debt (not just student loans) require a new statute or an amendment to the existing programs?

5. Reform / limit insurance prior authorization (including AI-driven denials).

  • Status: Tried; partially done in 2025
  • The record: 2025 was the breakthrough-and-veto year. What passed: AB463 (Backus D) tightened prior-authorization rules and deadlines for private insurers, Medicaid, and the Children’s Health Insurance Program - 42-0 and 20-0, now law. What was vetoed: SB128 (Neal D), barring insurers from relying solely on artificial intelligence to deny prior-authorization requests - the AI-denial concern participants raised - passed the Assembly 23-16 and the Senate 15-6 and was vetoed. AB290 (Nguyen D), the bill participants named as having died and being planned for reintroduction, cleared Assembly Commerce and Labor with amendments, was re-referred, and died without further action at session’s end; three other 2025 prior-auth bills (AB295, AB470, SB398 - two of them Republican-led) died in their first committees. The earlier record: all three 2019 attempts died in committee (AB225, AB372, SB359), while the adjacent step-therapy limits passed unanimously in 2023 (SB167, SB194) and dental-denial review by a dentist passed in 2025 (AB202, 42-0 and 20-0).
  • Where the decision sits: State law reaches state-regulated plans, Medicaid, and CHIP - AB463 covers exactly that set. The record does not show what the SB128 veto objected to (no veto messages in this dataset).
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2025 AB463 Tightened prior authorization rules for private insurers, Medicaid, and the Children’s Health Insurance Program. Enacted Became law
2025 SB128 Barred insurers from relying solely on artificial intelligence to deny prior authorization requests. Vetoed Vetoed by the governor
2025 AB290 Added rules and deadlines for insurers requiring prior authorization before covering medical or dental care. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 AB295 Set faster deadlines for insurers, Medicaid, and CHIP to decide prior authorization requests. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 AB470 Added requirements on insurers’ use of prior authorization for medical and dental care. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB398 Add rules limiting how health insurers use prior authorization for medical and dental care. Failed Died in its first committee
2019 AB225 Set rules health plans must follow when requiring prior authorization before paying for care. Failed Died in its first committee
2019 AB372 Required insurers to cover emergency care without prior authorization, even from out-of-network providers. Failed Died in its first committee
2019 SB359 Stopped insurers from requiring new prior authorization for chronic condition treatments already approved. Failed Died in its first committee
2023 SB167 Blocks insurers from forcing patients to retry cheaper drugs (step therapy) in certain cases. Enacted Became law
2023 SB194 Expands patients’ ability to get exemptions from insurer step-therapy drug requirements. Enacted Became law
2025 AB202 Required a dentist, not just a physician, to make dental coverage denial decisions for managed care plans. Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - AB463 is new law - which of AB290’s provisions did it absorb, and what remains for a refile? - What would the AI-denial ban need to change to survive the desk, and does its 23-16 Assembly margin leave room for an override strategy?

6. Reduce administrative burden and eliminate insurance middlemen, including pharmacy benefit managers.

  • Status: Tried repeatedly; stalled late
  • The record: The transparency layer is law; the regulation layer keeps dying within sight of the finish. Enacted: 2019 AB141 (Republican-carried) barred PBMs from gagging pharmacists who tell patients about cheaper options; 2019 SB276 ordered the drug-cost and rebate study; 2019 SB378 made Medicaid drug contractors pass rebates back to the state; 2023 AB434 protected 340B providers from PBM penalties; and 2025 SB389 (Scheible D + Stone R) put Medicaid prescription drugs under a single state-contracted PBM, 41-0 and 18-2. The failures escalate in reach and die later: licensing PBMs under the state health department died in committee in 2021 (SB392); making PBMs follow the same rules as the insurers they serve passed the Senate 21-0 in 2023 (SB352, Scheible D + Buck R) and died in the Assembly; the 2025 rewrite SB316 (Nguyen D + Stone R + Titus R) passed the Senate 18-2 and the Assembly 42-0 and died on the final day without concurrence; and the two committee-stage retries (SB149, SB209) died in 2025. No bill in this record proposes eliminating middlemen outright - every design regulates, licenses, or consolidates them.
  • Where the decision sits: State law reaches PBMs serving state-regulated plans and Medicaid; the enacted 2025 design consolidates Medicaid pharmacy middlemen into one audited contract rather than removing the function.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 AB141 Stopped pharmacy benefit managers from restricting pharmacists who tell patients about cheaper drug options. Enacted Became law
2019 SB276 Ordered a legislative study of prescription drug costs and how manufacturer rebates affect prices. Enacted Became law
2019 SB378 Required Medicaid drug contractors to share information and pass drug rebates back to the state. Enacted Became law
2023 AB434 Stopped pharmacy benefit managers and insurers from penalizing providers in the 340B drug discount program. Enacted Became law
2025 SB389 Require the state to contract with a single pharmacy benefit manager for Medicaid drug benefits by 2030. Enacted Became law
2021 SB392 Requires pharmacy benefit managers to be licensed and regulated by the state health department. Failed Died in its first committee
2023 SB352 Makes pharmacy benefit managers follow the same insurance rules as the insurers they serve. Failed Died in the second house
2025 SB316 Expand pharmacy benefit manager regulation to also cover insurance plans for pharmacist services. Failed Passed both houses, still no law
2025 SB149 Require the state to use a single pharmacy benefit manager for Medicaid prescription drug coverage. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB209 Tighten state regulation of pharmacy benefit managers who administer prescription drug coverage. Failed Died in its first committee
2021 SB171 Bans pharmacy benefit managers from forcing mail-order drugs and certain copay accumulator programs. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2023 AB440 Tightened regulation of pharmacy benefit managers that run insurers’ prescription drug plans. Failed Died in its first committee

Open questions for a working group: - SB316 passed both houses and died on the calendar - is a clean refile the shortest path, and what did the final amendments change? - Does the single-PBM Medicaid contract (SB389) produce the savings its design assumes - and does that model extend to commercial plans?

7. Improve provider-network accuracy and speed up insurance credentialing.

  • Status: Barely tried
  • The record: This is the thinnest record of the ten proposals. What exists: 2019 SB234 requires insurers to use a standardized letter and report trends when they reject a provider’s application to join a network - passed 21-0 and 39-0, the one direct network-admission law in the set. 2023 SB494 funded Medicaid’s computer systems including centralized provider credentialing (enacted, 20-0 and 42-0) - an administrative modernization, not a mandate on insurers. No bill in this record sets credentialing deadlines for insurers, audits network-directory accuracy, or defines ‘full’ panels. The adjacent attempts died: 2019 SB290 (Hardy R, network plans paying out-of-network providers for unanticipated care) died in its first committee, and 2021 SB90 (Hardy R, keeping unfounded investigations from counting against providers in credentialing) passed the Senate 21-0 and died in the Assembly.
  • Where the decision sits: Split lever: state law can regulate credentialing of state-licensed plans (SB234 shows the form); network contracts and panel-size decisions are otherwise private contracting, and Medicaid credentialing runs through the state’s own systems (SB494).
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 SB234 Made insurers use a standard letter and report trends when rejecting doctors from their provider networks. Enacted Became law
2023 SB494 Funds Medicaid agency computer systems, including utilization review and centralized provider credentialing. Enacted Became law
2019 SB290 Made insurer network plans pay out-of-network providers for unanticipated care, addressing surprise bills. Failed Died in its first committee
2021 SB90 Relabeled unfounded investigations of health providers so they do not count against licensure, credentialing or insurance. Failed Died in the second house

Open questions for a working group: - A credentialing-deadline or directory-accuracy mandate has never been filed - what would its committee path be, Commerce and Labor or Health and Human Services? - Did SB234’s rejection-letter and trend-reporting data ever get analyzed, and does it show the ‘full panel’ pattern participants described?

8. Expand insurance pooling for small businesses and non-employer groups.

  • Status: Precedent exists
  • The record: Nevada has legislated in this space in every direction except the one participants sketched. 2019 SB481 set state rules for association health plans and self-funded multiple-employer arrangements offering small-business coverage (passed 21-0 and 39-0). 2021 SB396 let public agencies pool with private entities to purchase prescription drugs and medical supplies (21-0 and 40-1). The biggest structure is the Public Option: 2021 SB420 (Cannizzaro D) created a state-designed plan any Nevadan can buy - the record’s rare contested enactment, 12-9 in the Senate and 26-15 in the Assembly. A 2019 study resolution (SCR10) on a public health-coverage plan was adopted. What died: the 2019 state bulk drug-purchasing list (SB226, Spearman D) in its first committee. No bill in this record creates an any-group pooling mechanism - the association/credit-union design participants floated has never been filed.
  • Where the decision sits: Partly a state lever: the record’s bills regulate state-licensed association plans and state purchasing. The employer-based structure participants called the key obstacle is federal tax and benefits law; no state bill in this record can or does repeal it.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 SB481 Regulated association health plans and self-funded multiple employer arrangements offering small-employer coverage. Enacted Became law
2021 SB396 Lets public agencies join with private entities to purchase prescription drugs and medical supplies. Enacted Became law
2021 SB420 Creates a state-designed Public Option health insurance plan for Nevadans to buy. Enacted Became law
2019 SCR10 Ordered a study of creating a public health insurance plan open to all Nevada residents. Unknown Died in its first committee
2019 SB226 Created a shared state prescription drug list and bulk purchasing to lower drug costs for public health plans. Failed Died in its first committee
2019 SB77 Eased competitive bidding rules so county hospitals can buy supplies through group purchasing contracts. Enacted Became law

Open questions for a working group: - The Public Option exists in statute - what is its implementation status, and does it serve the small-business buyers participants described? - Would an any-group pool be regulated under SB481’s association-plan framework, or need a new one?

9. Increase insurance reimbursement rates to attract and retain providers.

  • Status: Tried; narrow raises pass, broad reform stalls
  • The record: The record splits cleanly by scope. Targeted Medicaid raises become law, usually unanimously: autism behavior-technician rates to at least $52/hour (2021 SB96), letting provider-assessment money fund higher Medicaid payments to more provider types (2023 SB435, after the Assembly version AB197 died on the origin floor), special billing categories and rates for children’s cancer clinics (2023 SB221) and mental-health training clinics (2025 SB353), family-caregiver payment (2025 SB185, 41-1 and 21-0), epilepsy-device rates (2025 AB284), and dropping the out-of-state cap on autism rates (2025 SB497). Systemic reform dies at the money stage: the 2019 study of paying Medicaid rates that reflect actual provider costs (AB116) died on the origin floor, the 2023 rate-review overhaul (AB99) died the same way, across-the-board children’s rate raises died in committee twice (2023 SB255 and 2025 SB239, both Buck R), the 2025 substance-use rate raise (SB150) and the mental-health rate resolution (SB366, Krasner R) died in committee, and the payment-rate study (2021 AB347) died on the floor. On the commercial side, vision-plan contract-and-reimbursement rules passed twice (2021 AB436, 2025 AB448 - Koenig R). The backdrop: the 31st (2020) Special Session’s budget bill AB3 cut FY2020-2021 appropriations including Department of Health and Human Services budgets (passed 36-6 and 21-0).
  • Where the decision sits: Split lever: the Legislature sets Medicaid rates through the budget; commercial reimbursement is contractual, and the record’s commercial bills regulate contract terms (vision plans) rather than setting rates. Medicare and Tricare rates - the examples participants used - are federal and appear in no Nevada bill.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2021 SB96 Raised Medicaid and Check-Up payment rates for autism behavior technicians to at least $52 hourly. Enacted Became law
2023 AB197 Let the state assess certain health providers to fund higher Medicaid payments to them. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2023 SB435 Lets provider-assessment money fund higher Medicaid payments to more care providers. Enacted Became law
2023 SB221 Creates a special Medicaid billing category and rates for clinics treating children with cancer and rare diseases. Enacted Became law
2025 SB353 Create a Medicaid billing category and payment rates for clinics that train mental health students. Enacted Became law
2025 SB185 Let Medicaid pay family members who provide personal care to disabled or chronically ill children. Enacted Became law
2025 AB284 Sought higher Medicaid reimbursement rates for devices that treat epilepsy. Enacted Became law
2025 SB497 Drop the rule that Medicaid autism therapy rates must match other states’ rates. Enacted Became law
2019 AB116 Ordered a study of what raising Medicaid payment rates to reflect actual provider costs would cost. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2023 AB99 Changes how Medicaid payment rates to health providers are reviewed and updated. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2023 SB255 Raises Medicaid payment rates at least 10 percent for services mainly treating seriously ill children. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB239 Raise Medicaid payment rates for services treating children with cancer and serious childhood diseases. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB150 Raise Medicaid payment rates for certain substance use disorder treatment services. Failed Died in its first committee
2025 SB366 Ask the federal government to raise Medicaid payment rates for mental health and therapy providers. Failed Died in its first committee
2021 AB347 Orders a legislative study of health care payment rates and their impact on patients and costs. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2021 AB436 Restricts vision insurers’ contract terms with eye-care providers and requires reimbursement disclosures. Enacted Became law
2025 AB448 Restricted how insurers may set reimbursement and contract terms with vision care providers. Enacted Became law
2019 SB484 Required Medicaid to reimburse chiropractor services. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 SB118 Required Medicaid to cover and fairly reimburse pharmacist-provided services within their scope of practice. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar

Open questions for a working group: - Is the binding constraint statute or the biennial budget - and would the 2019 AB116-style cost study change the budget conversation? - The provider-assessment mechanism (2023 SB435) funds rate raises without General Fund money - which provider types could it extend to?

10. Right-size the care setting (urgent care vs. ER) and use rotating/staffed clinics for access.

  • Status: No bill on the specific model
  • The record: The split-billing model participants designed - separating ER from urgent care so minor issues don’t incur ER charges, with rotating physicians or a state-paid physician per county - has never been filed in this record. What exists around its edges: the 2019 out-of-network emergency billing law (AB469, 38-3 and 21-0) limits what out-of-network providers can bill patients for emergency care; 2023 SB497 adjusted the arbitration system for those payment disputes; 2019 AB317 made off-campus hospital sites offering urgent care or emergency care carry distinct provider identifiers; and 2023 AB277 (Koenig R) let small 24/7 rural emergency hospitals operate in Nevada, 41-0 and 21-0. The bills that tried to control what emergency facilities charge died early: 2023 AB85 (Orentlicher D, a state process to set hospital and emergency-center prices) died on the origin floor, and 2025 AB349 (Orentlicher D, capping charges for public-employee plans at Medicare multiples) died in its first committee. Telehealth - the other access lever participants debated - is largely settled law: 2021 SB5 modernized coverage, 2023 SB119 kept payment parity, and 2023 AB276 extended it to provider-to-provider consultation.
  • Where the decision sits: Mixed lever: Nevada licenses facility types (AB277 shows the form) and regulates billing of state-regulated plans, but ER billing practice also runs through federal Medicare rules - 2019 AB232 (requiring most hospitals to join Medicare, obligating emergency care regardless of ability to pay) is where the record touches that layer. County-level staffed clinics would be a county budget question no bill in this record addresses.
Bill What it tried Result Where it ended
2019 AB469 Regulated how out-of-network providers bill patients for medically necessary emergency services. Enacted Became law
2023 SB497 Adjusts arbitration rules for payment disputes over out-of-network emergency care. Enacted Became law
2019 AB317 Required off-campus hospital sites offering surgery, urgent or emergency care to use separate provider identifiers. Enacted Became law
2023 AB277 Allowed rural emergency hospitals, small 24/7 emergency facilities, to operate in Nevada. Enacted Became law
2023 AB85 Creates a process to set fixed prices hospitals, emergency centers and surgery centers can charge. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2025 AB349 Capped what hospitals and emergency centers may charge patients covered by public employee health plans. Failed Died in its first committee
2021 SB5 Updates telehealth rules, including which technologies count and how services are covered. Enacted Became law
2023 SB119 Keeps requiring insurers to pay for telehealth visits the same as in-person care. Enacted Became law
2023 AB276 Broadened telehealth to include provider-to-provider consultations, including for forensic medical exams. Enacted Became law
2019 AB232 Required most hospitals to join Medicare, obligating emergency care regardless of patients’ ability to pay. Enacted Became law
2021 SB56 Requires insurers to cover behavioral health services delivered through telehealth like in-person care. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar
2021 SB326 Revises provider licensing rules for telehealth care following pandemic emergency license waivers. Failed Died on its first floor / calendar

Open questions for a working group: - Would the split-billing model be a facility-licensing bill (like AB277) or an insurance-billing bill (like AB469) - and which committee owns each? - The price-control versions died twice under one sponsor - is the design or the scope (all payers vs. public employees) what stopped them? - What does the state-paid-county-physician idea cost, and has any interim committee ever scoped it?

Appendix I — Sources and review notes

This appendix holds the reviewer-facing material that does not belong in the front brief: the claim-to-source mapping, data-collection notes, and review status.

How this record was assembled

Bills were discovered two ways for the 2019, 2021, 2023, and 2025 sessions: NELIS full-text search (health care, health insurance, health benefit plan, managed care, public option, licensure compact, licensure by endorsement, physician, nurse, nursing, physician assistant, medical education, graduate medical education, residency, health care workforce, loan repayment, student loan, Health Service Corps, prior authorization, utilization review, step therapy, pharmacy benefit, pharmacist, prescription drug, network plan, provider network, network adequacy, credentialing, small employer, group health, Medicaid, Medicare, reimbursement, urgent care, emergency room, emergency department, telehealth, telemedicine, hospital, medical debt, medical bill, balance billing, out-of-network) and a full harvest of the Legislative Counsel Bureau’s official Subject Index of Bills under the healthcare heading family — INSURANCE (with the agent-licensing, guaranty, and premium-tax subsets excluded), INSURANCE COMPANIES and ADMINISTRATORS, HEALTH CARE PROVIDERS, HEALTH CARE — RESTRAINING COSTS OF, HEALTH CARE ACCESS AND RECRUITMENT GRANT PROGRAM, HEALTH BENEFIT PLANS, HEALTH MAINTENANCE ORGANIZATIONS, MANAGED CARE, NONPROFIT HOSPITAL/MEDICAL/DENTAL SERVICE CORPORATIONS, PREPAID LIMITED HEALTH SERVICE ORGANIZATIONS, PUBLIC OPTION HEALTH BENEFIT PLAN, SILVER STATE HEALTH INSURANCE EXCHANGE, PATIENT PROTECTION COMMISSION, PATIENTS, PHARMACY BENEFIT MANAGERS, PHARMACISTS AND PHARMACY, PHARMACEUTICAL SALES REPRESENTATIVES, PRESCRIPTIONS and the PRESCRIPTION DRUG AFFORDABILITY bodies, REBATES — PRESCRIPTION DRUGS, MEDICAID, MEDICARE, MEDICAL BILLS, MEDICAL DEBT, HOSPITALS, COUNTY HOSPITALS, TELEHEALTH, PHYSICIANS, PHYSICIAN ASSISTANTS, PHYSICIAN VISA WAIVER PROGRAM, OSTEOPATHIC PHYSICIANS, NURSES AND NURSING, NURSING POOLS, MEDICAL EDUCATION COUNCIL, HEALTH SERVICE CORPS, EMERGENCY MEDICAL SERVICES, and PSYCHOLOGY INTERJURISDICTIONAL COMPACT — with entry-level keywords harvested from every heading (licensure compact, nurse licensure, interjurisdictional compact, licensure by endorsement, prior authorization, utilization review, step therapy, pharmacy benefit, graduate medical education, residency program, loan repayment, health service corps, network adequacy, provider network, credentialing, urgent care, emergency room, freestanding emergency, telehealth, telemedicine, medical debt, surprise billing, balance billing, out-of-network, health insurance, health benefit plan, health coverage, reimbursement rate, public option).

All six 2020–2025 special sessions were checked by hand; two healthcare-relevant special-session bills exist and are documented with full history and votes in sources/nevada/cost-of-living/verification/special-sessions.json: the 31st (2020) Special Session AB3 (the pandemic budget bill, which reduced FY2020–2021 appropriations including Department of Health and Human Services budgets and authorized DHHS budget transfers; enacted 36–6 and 21–0, Chapter 5) and the 36th (2025) Special Session SB5 (the Statewide Health Care Access and Recruitment Grant Program under the Nevada Health Authority; enacted 15–6 and 37–0, Chapter 12). SB5’s digest names the same program the regular session’s SB434 created before dying on concurrence — the linkage the front brief reports. Reviewed and set aside as not healthcare-cost relevant: 32nd (2020) SB4 and SCR1, 36th (2025) SB3 and SB7 (notes in the same file).

The search yielded 772 bills, hand-curated (every NELIS digest read) into 409 policy bills — 276 core and 133 adjacent, the basis for all headline numbers — and 363 context bills kept for audit. This is broad, double-source coverage but not a provably complete universe; the record shows where each bill stopped, never why (no veto messages or floor debate). Committee Yea votes are partly inferred (committee membership minus recorded Nay/Absent) because Nevada minutes usually list only No and Absent votes; those rows are marked in the source data. Party labels come from official NELIS legislator rosters (97.7% of roll-call ballot rows matched; the unmatched rows are minutes-parsing name fragments left unlabeled). Sponsor party coverage is 100%: three sponsor names missing from the session rosters (Assemblyman Michael Sprinkle, Senator Kelvin Atkinson, Assemblyman C.H. Miller) were resolved from their official NELIS legislator pages and marked party_source: nelis_legislator_page in processed/bill-sponsors.json. Two generated provenance strings inside evidence-pack.json (discovery_note and one data-limits line) and the static intro paragraphs of Appendices A, F, H, and README were corrected after the build to describe this issue’s headings and coverage; all counts are untouched pipeline output. Every factual claim in the reality map and front brief was checked programmatically against the evidence pack before writing (working/nevada/cost-of-living/fact-check-reality-map.py — all claims verified).

Constituent-statement sources

The ten proposals, their consensus notes, and trade-offs come from the Phase 2 Community Conversation RAG dataset (“NV1 - RAG - Phase 2 Constituent Input”, NV1 - Cost of Living sheet, June–July 2026), encoded in config/issues/nevada-cost-of-living.yaml → constituent_proposals. Participant quotations in the working files (“why get a prior authorization when the doctor’s gonna say I need it anyway?”, “a robot”) are from that dataset, not from legislative records. The scope note from the same sheet (tax incentives for employer coverage, ACA navigator outreach, and specialty-targeting via health assessments surfaced but with less consensus) is preserved as comments in the issue config.

Statute and agency references

NRS chapters most relevant to this issue (URLs in the issue config): 422 (Medicaid), 439A (health planning and costs), 449 (medical facilities), 630 (physicians), 632 (nursing), 683A (insurance administrators and PBMs), 695G (managed care). Agency starting points: Division of Health Care Financing and Policy (dhcfp.nv.gov), Division of Insurance (doi.nv.gov), Patient Protection Commission (ppc.nv.gov).
